## Management Summary

### Verdict

🔴 The application carries eight Critical findings across authentication, data access, and session handling - any one of which gives an unauthenticated or authenticated attacker full account or database control without chaining.

**Risk distribution:** 🔴 Critical: 8 · 🟠 High: 22 · 🟡 Medium: 19 · 🟢 Low: n/a · **Total: 49**<br/>**Reporting threshold:** medium - Low and Informational excluded<br/>**Assessment evidence:** 44 confirmed-exploitable finding(s) · 2 implementation weakness(es) · 7 design weakness(es)


**Scope:** 7 of 8 components received full STRIDE analysis - the externally-reachable, authentication-bearing, and business-critical surface. 1 further component(s) received a reduced-budget screening pass (all six STRIDE categories, no verification greps) and are marked `Screened` in the component table. (see [§1 Scope](#scope)).


**Basis:** a code-derived threat model at implementation level - built from repository evidence, not a planning document. Design intent, business processes, runtime behaviour and production-only configuration are outside what this analysis can see (see [§11 Out of Scope](#11-out-of-scope)).

<br/>

**What an attacker can do today, worst first:**

<blockquote style="border-left: 3px solid #dc2626; background: #fef2f2; padding: 16px 20px; margin: 0;">

- **Admin account takeover without credentials** — Database query injection in the login route lets any internet visitor authenticate as any user, including administrators. *(🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL injection via raw query string interpolation (`routes/login.ts:34`) → [W-001](#w-001))*
- **Unauthorized session token forgery** — The token signing secret is committed to the source repository; any repository reader can issue tokens for any user or admin role. *(🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`), 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Algorithm Confusion via Missing Algorithm Allowlist → [W-003](#w-003))* — ✓ verified attack path
- **Server process code execution** — The product-review route passes user input to a database query that executes arbitrary server-side code. *(🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — NoSQL \$where injection enables JavaScript execution (`showProductReviews.ts:36`))*
- **Customer database exposed** — The product search route concatenates user input directly into a database query, exposing the entire data store to read or export. *(🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string `routes/search.ts:23` → [W-001](#w-001))*
- **Self-promotion to administrator** — Any logged-in user can promote their own account to admin by including a role field in the profile update request. *(🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body `routes/verify.ts:53`)* — ✓ verified attack path
- **Active login stolen via injected script** — Session tokens held in the browser script context are reachable by any injected page script; the stored cross-site scripting surface spans multiple sanitizer bypass points. *(🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — XSS via Angular Sanitizer Bypass, 🔴 [F-027](#f-027) — JWT bearer token stored in localStorage (`Services/request.interceptor.ts:13`))* — ✓ verified attack path

</blockquote>

<br/>

No single perimeter control prevents these paths. Query hardening, secret rotation, and moving session tokens out of the script context are the mandatory first repairs.

### Top Weaknesses

The systemic root-cause problems behind the findings - the classes to fix, not just individual bugs. Each links to the full [Weakness Register](#7-weakness-register) with its findings and remediation.

- 🔴 **[W-001](#w-001) - Database access relies on concatenated queries** (Critical) - Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. _Proven by [F-005](#f-005), [F-006](#f-006), [F-008](#f-008)._
- 🔴 **[W-002](#w-002) - `Authorization` is implemented route by route** (Critical) - `Authorization` depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces role, ownership, and tenant scope. _Proven by 🔴 [F-007](#f-007), 🔴 [F-032](#f-032), 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) (+3 more)._
- 🔴 **[W-003](#w-003) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store** (Critical) - Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or `config` rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository r… _Proven by 🔴 [F-010](#f-010), 🔴 [F-022](#f-022), 🔴 [F-030](#f-030)._
- 🟠 **[W-004](#w-004) - Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication** (High) - Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. _Proven by [F-016](#f-016), [F-023](#f-023), [F-046](#f-046)._
- 🟠 **[W-005](#w-005) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation** (High) - Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting select… _Proven by [F-017](#f-017)._
- 🟠 **[W-006](#w-006) - Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references** (High) - CI/CD workflows resolve third-party actions and other build dependencies to mutable tags or branches instead of immutable commit digests, so a retagged or compromised upstream runs inside the pipel… _Proven by [F-020](#f-020)._

### Security Posture & Top Threats

**Figure 1 - Architecture & Top Threats**

Data-flow diagram: external entities, processes and data stores in their trust zones (Internet → Application → Data), the data flows between them, and the attack scenarios numbered as in the table below. Each crossing of a dashed trust-boundary line carries the `tb-N` id catalogued in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries) with the verdict on its enforcement assumption. The in-figure legend on the right explains the notation.

![Figure 1 - Architecture & Top Threats](threat-model-juice-shop-standard-v0.6.0b3.figure1.svg)

**Figure 2 - Risk Flow: Actor → Tier → Impact**

Heatmap: **actors** (left) → **architecture tiers** (middle, Client → Application → Data) → **impact** (right). Numbered red arrows ①–⑥ are the threats enumerated in the Top Threats table below.

![Figure 2 - Risk Flow: Actor to Tier to Impact](threat-model-juice-shop-standard-v0.6.0b3.figure2.svg)

**Threat actors.** The actors below drive the numbered attack paths in the figures above. The **Shop User** is the *victim* of client-side attacks (XSS / CSRF), not an attacker - in Figure 2 the compromise surfaces as the resulting business-impact node rather than as a separate actor box.

- **Shop User** — legitimate customer; target of client-side attacks; target of ④ Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting.
- **Anonymous Internet Attacker** — no account or prior foothold; drives ① Insecure Query Construction & Data Access, ② Hardcoded Secrets & Weak Cryptography, ⑤ Sensitive File & Secret Exposure.
- **Authenticated Internet Attacker** — owns a regular account; logged in; drives ③ Broken `Authorization` & Access Control, ⑥ Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval).

**6 structural threats**, grouped by weakness class - each row is one threat, not one finding. *Threat Description* states the general architectural weakness (STRIDE in brackets); *Findings* lists the concrete instances, each linked to [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register) with its component; *Risk & Impact* combines severity with business consequence.

| <a id="path-cross-site-scripting"></a>④ | **Output Encoding / Cross-Site Scripting** _(T·I)_<br/>Angular sanitizer bypass calls allow stored and DOM-based XSS payloads that execute in authenticated victim sessions and exfiltrate `localStorage` JWT tokens. | <span style="white-space:nowrap">🔴&nbsp;[F-002](#f-002)</span> - XSS via Angular Sanitizer Bypass (`search-result.component.ts:143`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-01](#c-01)</span>&nbsp;Angular SPA Frontend<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🔴&nbsp;[F-027](#f-027)</span> - JWT bearer token stored in `localStorage` (`request.interceptor.ts:13`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-01](#c-01)</span>&nbsp;Angular SPA Frontend<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-043](#f-043)</span> - JWT Stored in `localStorage` Exposed to XSS Theft (`login.component.ts:101`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-03](#c-03)</span>&nbsp;Authentication and Session Management | 🔴 **Critical**<br/>Customer Session Hijack · Customer Data Exfiltration | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-002](#m-002)</span> — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-026](#m-026)</span> — Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies |
| <a id="path-sensitive-data-exposure"></a>⑤ | **Sensitive File & Secret Exposure** _(I)_<br/>`MD5`-without-salt password hashes and cleartext payment card integers in the database let any attacker who reads the database file recover credentials and financial data offline. | <span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-017](#f-017)</span> - Path traversal filesystem access from request input (`dataErasure.ts:104`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express REST API Backend<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-024](#f-024)</span> - `MD5` Password Hash Without Salt Enables Offline Recovery (`insecurity.ts:41`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-03](#c-03)</span>&nbsp;Authentication and Session Management<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-025](#f-025)</span> - Payment card number stored as cleartext INTEGER (`card.ts:36`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express REST API Backend<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-026](#f-026)</span> - Unsalted `MD5` hashes expose entire credential store (`insecurity.ts:41`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-04](#c-04)</span>&nbsp;Persistent Data Store (SQLite + MarsDB)<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-028](#f-028)</span> - Unauthorized Information Broadcast (`registerWebsocketEvents.ts:29`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-05](#c-05)</span>&nbsp;Socket\.IO Real-Time Server<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟡&nbsp;[F-042](#f-042)</span> - Inverted tool-call visibility guard leaks LLM internals to non-admins (`chat.ts:225`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express REST API Backend | 🟠 **High**<br/>Customer Data Exfiltration | <span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-017](#m-017)</span> — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-023](#m-023)</span> — Hash passwords with a strong, salted algorithm |
| <a id="path-remote-code-execution"></a>⑥ | **Remote Code Execution (unsafe eval)** _(E)_<br/>Server-side template injection via eval on a stored username field and NoSQL JavaScript execution in the product-review route expose the server process to arbitrary code execution. | <span style="white-space:nowrap">🔴&nbsp;[F-009](#f-009)</span> - NoSQL \`$where` injection enables JavaScript execution (`showProductReviews.ts:36`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express REST API Backend<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-019](#f-019)</span> - Input compiled as template source (`userProfile.ts:87`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express REST API Backend<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🔴&nbsp;[F-034](#f-034)</span> - Code execution via eval on stored username (`userProfile.ts:61`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express REST API Backend<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">🟠&nbsp;[F-049](#f-049)</span> - Prompt injection bypasses coupon discount cap via unconstrained schema (`chat.ts:179`) <span style="white-space:nowrap">→&nbsp;[C-02](#c-02)</span>&nbsp;Express REST API Backend | 🔴 **Critical**<br/>Full Server Compromise | <span style="white-space:nowrap">● [M-009](#m-009)</span> — Use parameterized database queries<br/><span style="white-space:nowrap">◕ [M-018](#m-018)</span> — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input |

| **2** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ● [M-009](#m-009) — Use parameterized database queries (`showProductReviews.ts:36`) | 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — NoSQL \$where injection enables JavaScript execution (`showProductReviews.ts`) | Low |
| **3** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ● [M-006](#m-006) — Use parameterized database queries (`search.ts:23`) | 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string (`routes/search.ts`) | Medium |
| **4** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ● [M-007](#m-007) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (`address.ts:11`) | 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — Insecure Direct Object Reference (`routes/address.ts`) | Medium |
| **5** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ● [M-010](#m-010) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (`insecurity.ts:21`) | 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts`) | Medium |
| **6** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ● [M-011](#m-011) — Allowlist client-controlled fields (`verify.ts:53`) | 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body (`routes/verify.ts`) | Medium |
| **7** | [C-03](#c-03) — Authentication and Session Management | ● [M-005](#m-005) — Use parameterized database queries (`login.ts:34`) | 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass (`routes/login.ts`) | Low |
| **8** | [C-03](#c-03) — Authentication and Session Management | ● [M-001](#m-001) — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification (`insecurity.ts:189`) | 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Algorithm Confusion via Missing Algorithm Allowlist (`lib/insecurity.ts`) | Medium |
| **9** | [C-01](#c-01) — Angular SPA Frontend | ◕ [M-002](#m-002) — Encode output instead of bypassing the framework sanitizer (`search-result.component.ts:143`) | 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — XSS via Angular Sanitizer Bypass (`frontend/src/app/search-result/search-result.component.ts`) | Low |
| **10** | [C-01](#c-01) — Angular SPA Frontend | ◕ [M-026](#m-026) — Store session tokens in HttpOnly, Secure cookies (`request.interceptor.ts:13`) | 🔴 [F-027](#f-027) — JWT bearer token stored in localStorage (`Services/request.interceptor.ts`) | High |
| **11** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ◕ [M-022](#m-022) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint (`server.ts:606`) | 🔴 [F-023](#f-023) — Unauthenticated admin configuration exposure (`server.ts`) | Low |
| **12** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ◕ [M-033](#m-033) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input (`userProfile.ts:61`) | 🔴 [F-034](#f-034) — Code execution via eval on stored username (`routes/userProfile.ts`) | Low |
| **13** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ◕ [M-028](#m-028) — Use parameterized database queries (`trackOrder.ts:18`) | 🔴 [F-029](#f-029) — NoSQL \$where JS injection returns all orders (`routes/trackOrder.ts`) | Medium |
| **14** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ◕ [M-031](#m-031) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint (`server.ts:310`) | 🔴 [F-032](#f-032) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware (`server.ts`) | Medium |
| **15** | [C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend | ◕ [M-034](#m-034) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint (`updateProductReviews.ts:18`) | 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Missing ownership check enables cross-user bulk review update (`routes/updateProductReviews.ts`) | Medium |
| **16** | [C-03](#c-03) — Authentication and Session Management | ◕ [M-021](#m-021) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store (`insecurity.ts:42`) | 🔴 [F-022](#f-022) — Hardcoded HMAC Secret Enables Arbitrary Password Reset (`lib/insecurity.ts`) | Low |
| **17** | [C-07](#c-07) — Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface | ◕ [M-029](#m-029) — Move secrets to a managed secret store (`checkKeys.ts:10`) | 🔴 [F-030](#f-030) — Hardcoded BIP39 mnemonic exposes all derived wallet keys (`checkKeys.ts`) | Low |

*15 additional P1/P2 mitigations capped from the leader-board · 18 P3 backlog items in [§10 Mitigation Register](#10-mitigation-register). Sorted by priority (P1 first), then component, then leverage (most findings first), severity (Critical first), and effort (Low first).*

### AI / LLM Exposure

The application exposes an LLM chat endpoint at `/rest/chat` that accepts unconstrained user input and returns model-generated output without server-side output validation or per-user rate limiting. See **[§6 Security Architecture](#6-security-architecture)** for the per-control detail.



- **[LLM01](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01/) Prompt Injection** — The chat route at `server.ts:638` forwards user-supplied text directly to the model without an injection-resistant system-prompt boundary; a crafted prompt can override the model's coupon-cap constraint and trigger unauthorized discount application. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend)_
  - ↳ 🟠 [F-049](#f-049) — Prompt injection bypasses coupon discount cap via unconstrained schema (`chat.ts:179`)

- **[LLM02](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm02/) Sensitive Information Disclosure** — The tool-call visibility guard in the chat route is inverted, so non-admin users receive internal LLM tool-call details and intermediate reasoning that should be visible only to administrators. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-042](#f-042) — Inverted tool-call visibility guard leaks LLM internals to non-admins (`chat.ts:225`)

- **[LLM10](https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm10/) Unbounded Consumption** — No per-user rate limit is enforced on the LLM chat endpoint at `server.ts:638`, allowing any authenticated user to issue unlimited model inference requests and drive up compute cost or exhaust API quota. _([C-02](#c-02) — Express REST API Backend)_
  - ↳ 🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — No per-user rate limit on LLM chat endpoint (`server.ts:638`)

### Operational Strengths

Operational controls rated Adequate or Partial - grouped into broad clusters (full per-control breakdown in [§6](#6-security-architecture)). Clusters demoted to Weak by open Critical/High findings appear in [§6](#6-security-architecture) instead, not here.

<table style="table-layout:fixed;width:100%">
<colgroup><col width="20%" style="width:20%"><col width="33%" style="width:33%"><col width="47%" style="width:47%"></colgroup>
<thead><tr><th>Strength</th><th>What's in Place</th><th>Effectiveness</th></tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Hardened HTTP Stack</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Browser-facing HTTP hardening - security headers, cookie flags, cross-origin policy, and abuse-protection limits.</em><br/>CORS Policy</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial - Coverage incomplete - see <a href="#7-weakness-register">§7</a> control assessment.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Observability &amp; Audit</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Runtime visibility - access logging, audit trails, and operational telemetry for post-incident review.</em><br/>Security Event Logging</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial - Coverage incomplete - see <a href="#7-weakness-register">§7</a> control assessment.</td></tr>
<tr><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><strong>Cryptographic Hygiene</strong></td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word"><em>Transport security, key lifecycle, and password-hash strength.</em><br/>Transport Encryption (TLS)</td><td style="overflow-wrap:break-word">⚠️ Partial - Bypassed by 1 High finding(s) of the kind this cluster is supposed to prevent - e.g.<br/>🟠 <a href="#f-024">F-024</a> — MD5 Password Hash Without Salt Enables Offline Recovery (<code>lib/insecurity.ts:41</code>).</td></tr>
</tbody>
</table>


**Bottom line:** These controls narrow specific attack surfaces but none eliminates a Critical finding on its own.

## 1. System Overview

**Repository:** `https://github.com/juice-shop/juice-shop.git`

### Scope

This threat model covers 8 components of juice-shop: **Angular SPA Frontend**, **Express REST API Backend**, **Authentication and Session Management**, **Persistent Data Store (SQLite + MarsDB)**, **Socket\.IO Real-Time Server**, **CI/CD Pipeline**, **Web3 / Wallet / NFT Surface**, **`marsdb` Embedded Store**.

All 8 modeled components received full STRIDE threat analysis.

**Out of scope:** third-party hosted dependencies, browser runtime, operating-system kernel, and the underlying network infrastructure.

**Basis:** a code-derived threat model at implementation level, built from source, configuration and git history. It describes the system as built, not as designed.

---

### Trust Boundaries

Canonical boundary crossings. **Assumption & verdict** names the condition that makes each crossing safe and whether this report still supports it, judged one condition at a time - which conditions apply follows from the direction of the crossing.

</tbody>
</table>

_Exposure, most exposed first: ⚠ Review (endpoints unresolved) · 🌐 External (reached from outside the model) · ◐ Unverified (crossing not confirmed) · 🔒 Internal (both ends modelled) · ↗ Egress (reaches outside the model)._

_Kind - the crossing mechanism (build pipeline, in-process, network), then after `·` what changes across it: data-origin, identity, operator, privilege, tenant. Shown alone, nothing changes._

_Conditions - **inbound**: validation, authentication, authorization · **outbound**: egress content, egress destination, response trust (replies are untrusted input) · **internal**: query construction (data never becomes program text), authentication, authorization._

_Verdict per condition - **broken**: a linked finding proves the gap · in doubt: related findings, none linked here · not examined: nothing bears on it. `N related` counts further findings on the same condition. Linked findings sit under the condition they break, or under _Unattributed_. A link raises effective severity only at a confirmed internet ingress; raw risk never changes._

_Identical on every row, so stated once here instead of in a column: source `detected` (derived from inspected repository evidence); status `resolved`. Any row that deviates is shown in the table._

## 2. Architecture Diagrams

### 2.1 System Context

Who interacts with juice-shop from the outside, and through which channels. Solid arrows show normal usage; dashed red arrows mark unauthenticated probing or exploit paths (C4 Level 1).

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    USER["End User<br/>(browser)"]
    ATTACKER["Anonymous<br/>Internet Attacker"]
    ADMIN["Admin User"]
    subgraph TBEDGE["Trust boundary · external → juice-shop-api (tb-1)<br/>external → juice-shop-auth (tb-3)<br/>+2 more"]
        SYSTEM["juice-shop"]
    end
    USER -->|HTTPS · normal usage| SYSTEM
    ATTACKER -.->|HTTPS · probing / exploit| SYSTEM
    ADMIN -->|HTTPS · admin actions| SYSTEM
    classDef user     fill:#e8f1ea,stroke:#2e7d32,color:#1b5e20,stroke-width:1.5px
    classDef attacker fill:#f3dada,stroke:#b71c1c,color:#7f0000,stroke-width:2px
    classDef admin    fill:#fef3c7,stroke:#b45309,color:#78350f,stroke-width:1.5px
    classDef sys      fill:#f2f2f2,stroke:#424242,color:#111,stroke-width:1.5px
    class USER user
    class ATTACKER attacker
    class ADMIN admin
    class SYSTEM sys
```

*Trust boundaries not named above: external → juice-shop-realtime (tb-2), external → ci-cd-pipeline (tb-4), juice-shop-auth → juice-shop-db (tb-5), juice-shop-api → juice-shop-db (tb-6), +1 more - every boundary is listed in [§1 Trust Boundaries](#trust-boundaries).*

**Key takeaway:** Every actor in the context interacts with juice-shop through its external interface, so authentication and input validation at that edge govern the entire attack surface.

## 3. Attack Walkthroughs

This section walks through how the **8 highest-priority of 18 Critical findings** are exploited - selected by severity, chain relevance, and threat-category diversity, with Access Control and LLM Abuse represented when present. Each walkthrough has attack steps, a focused sequence diagram, and the primary mitigation. How weaknesses combine toward the worst-case goal is in the [Critical Attack Tree](#critical-attack-tree); every other Critical, plus full per-finding context (severity rationale, assets, detection signals), is in the [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register).

### 3.1 JWT Algorithm Confusion in Authentication and Session Management

**Source:** 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — `lib/insecurity.ts:189`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-001](#f-001) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request targeting the weak spot at `lib/insecurity.ts:189`.
2. The attacker sends it; the missing control never rejects the crafted input.
3. Without an explicit algorithm allowlist, attackers can forge tokens with `alg:none` (older lib versions) or use the public key as an HMAC secret to mint valid signatures.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: juice-shop-auth - lib/insecurity.ts:189
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting lib/insecurity.ts line 189
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-347
    alt Current state — F-001
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-001
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-001 — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-001](#m-001) (Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification) lands, 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Algorithm Confusion via Missing Algorithm Allowlist is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:189` (Critical-severity, [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-001](#m-001) (Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification)

### 3.2 SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string

**Source:** 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — `routes/search.ts:23`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-006](#f-006) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Find the request parameter that reaches the raw query at `routes/search.ts:23`.
2. Interpolating request-controlled text into a SQL statement lets an attacker alter the query - exfiltrating or modifying arbitrary rows, bypassing authentication, or escalating to full database control.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: juice-shop-api - routes/search.ts:23
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: Crafted HTTP request to the affected endpoint with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-006
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-006
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-006 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-006](#m-006) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string `routes/search.ts:23` is exploitable at `routes/search.ts:23` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-006](#m-006) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.4 SQL Injection Authentication Bypass in Login

**Source:** 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Spoofing. See [§8 F-005](#f-005) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Attacker sends POST `/rest/user/login` with body `{"email":"' OR 1=1--","password":"x"}` to the unauthenticated endpoint.
2. The literal query becomes `SELECT * FROM Users` WHERE email = '`' OR 1=1--` AND ... which returns all rows; the first row is the admin account.
3. Server calls `security.authorize()` at `routes/login.ts:24` to issue a 6-hour `RS256` JWT for the returned user.
4. Attacker presents the issued JWT as a Bearer token and operates with full admin privileges on all subsequent API requests.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid

    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant API
    participant DB
    Note over API: juice-shop-auth - routes/login.ts:34
    Note over DB: Database
    Attacker->>API: POST /rest/user/login with classical OR 1=1 payload
    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-005
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-005
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-005 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-005](#m-005) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass (`routes/login.ts:34`) is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-005](#m-005) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.5 SQL injection in Login

**Source:** 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — `routes/login.ts:34`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-008](#f-008) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Attacker sends POST `/rest/user/login` with body `{"email":"' OR 1=1--","password":"x"}` - no prior authentication or token required.
2. `routes/login.ts:34` constructs SQL with the unescaped email: `WHERE email = '' OR 1=1--'`; the OR condition matches the first row (admin).
3. `afterLogin()` issues a JWT for the returned user record; attacker receives a valid admin-role JWT and basket ID in the response.
4. Attacker uses the token to access all authenticated API routes as admin, manipulate orders, and read all user records.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
    participant API
    participant DB

    Note over DB: Database

    API->>DB: SQL built by string interpolation - payload becomes query
    DB-->>API: First matching row regardless of intended predicate
    API-->>Attacker: 200 OK with authenticated session / leaked rows
    alt Current state — F-008
        Attacker->>API: The attacker sends the exploit for F-008
        API-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-008 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>API: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        API-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-008](#m-008) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL injection via raw query string interpolation (`routes/login.ts:34`) is exploitable at `routes/login.ts:34` (Critical-severity, [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-008](#m-008) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.6 NoSQL \$where injection enables JavaScript execution

**Source:** 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-943](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/943.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-009](#f-009) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Attacker sends GET `/rest/products/1`;`sleep(2000)``/reviews` without authentication - `server.ts:632` registers the endpoint with no `isAuthorized()` middleware.
2. `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36` builds `{ $where: 'this.product == 1;sleep(2000)' }` by direct string concatenation of the URL parameter.
3. MongoDB evaluates the JavaScript \`$where` expression; `global.sleep(2000)` at lines 17-25 blocks the Node\.js event loop for up to 2000ms per request.
4. Concurrent requests from an attacker starve the event loop, rendering all other endpoints including login, orders, and payment unresponsive.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: juice-shop-api - routes/showProductReviews.ts:36
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/showProductReviews.ts line 36
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-943
    alt Current state — F-009
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-009
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-009 — Use parameterized database queries
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-009](#m-009) (Use parameterized database queries) lands, 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — NoSQL \$where injection enables JavaScript execution (`showProductReviews.ts:36`) is exploitable at `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36` (Critical-severity, [CWE-943](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/943.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-009](#m-009) (Use parameterized database queries)

### 3.7 Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery

**Source:** 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — `lib/insecurity.ts:21`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)). STRIDE: Information Disclosure. See [§8 F-010](#f-010) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. Attacker clones or downloads the Juice Shop source repository and reads the 1024-bit RSA private key string literal at `lib/insecurity.ts:21`.
2. Attacker signs a JWT payload offline with the extracted private key using `RS256`, setting `role=admin` and a target user's id and email.
3. Attacker submits the forged token to any endpoint behind `security.isAuthorized()`; the server's `expressJwt` and `jwt.verify` functions accept it as legitimate.
4. Attacker accesses all user accounts, payment card data, and administrative functions without any prior credential.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant Repo
    participant App
    Note over Repo: Public repository
    Note over App: juice-shop-api - lib/insecurity.ts:21
    Attacker->>Repo: Clone or browse - extract private key from lib/insecurity.ts line 21
    Attacker->>Attacker: Sign forged token / signature with the extracted key
    Attacker->>App: Submit forged artefact in normal authentication flow
    App-->>Attacker: Request accepted as authentic - signature validates
    alt Current state — F-010
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker sends the exploit for F-010
        Repo-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-010 — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store
        Attacker->>Repo: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        Repo-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-010](#m-010) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store) lands, 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`) is exploitable at `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (Critical-severity, [CWE-321](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/321.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-010](#m-010) (Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store)

### 3.8 Insecure Direct Object Reference in Address

**Source:** 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — `routes/address.ts:11`

Severity **Critical** ([CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)). STRIDE: Tampering. See [§8 F-007](#f-007) for the full register row.

**Attack Steps**

1. An attacker crafts a request targeting the weak spot at `routes/address.ts:11`.
2. Server-side authorization MUST derive the resource owner from the authenticated session (`req.user` / `req.session` / `req.auth`), never from attacker-controlled request data.
3. Trusting `req.body.UserId` etc. enables horizontal privilege escalation across all authenticated tenants.

**Sequence Diagram**

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor Attacker
    participant App
    Note over App: juice-shop-api - routes/address.ts:11
    Attacker->>App: The attacker sends a crafted request targeting routes/address.ts line 11
    App->>App: Vulnerable branch executes without the missing control
    App-->>Attacker: The attacker reads the response confirming the weakness - CWE-639
    alt Current state — F-007
        Attacker->>App: The attacker sends the exploit for F-007
        App-->>Attacker: Exploit succeeds
    else After M-007 — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization
        Attacker->>App: The attacker retries the same request after the fix
        App-->>Attacker: Request rejected
    end
```

**Key takeaway:** Until ● [M-007](#m-007) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization) lands, 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — Insecure Direct Object Reference is exploitable at `routes/address.ts:11` (Critical-severity, [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html)).

**Defense in Depth**

- Primary mitigation: ● [M-007](#m-007) (Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization)

## 4. Assets

Information assets and the classification level that drives the Confidentiality / Integrity / Availability targets used in [§8 Findings Register](#8-findings-register) risk scoring.

| Asset | Classification | Description |
|----------------------|--------------|------------------------------------|
| Hardcoded Cryptographic Key Material | Restricted | RSA private key (1024-bit) and HMAC secret<br/>('pa4qacea4VK9t9nGv7yZtwmj') embedded<br/>verbatim in `lib/insecurity.ts`. These keys<br/>protect JWT integrity and coupon HMAC<br/>respectively; their exposure in the public<br/>repository negates all cryptographic<br/>protections. |
| Payment Card Records | Restricted | User-saved payment card entries (card<br/>number, expiry, card name) persisted in the<br/>SQLite Cards table. Processed by the payment<br/>routes. Leakage constitutes a PCI-relevant<br/>data breach even in a training context. |
| SQLite Database File | Restricted | Embedded SQLite file (data/juiceshop.sqlite<br/>or in-memory) holding all relational data:<br/>users (with password hashes), cards,<br/>addresses, baskets, orders, challenges. A<br/>single file-level read exposes all stored<br/>Restricted and Confidential data. |
| User Account Records | Confidential | Registered user records containing email<br/>address, username, `MD5`-hashed password, role<br/>flag, and profile data. Stored in the SQLite<br/>Users table via Sequelize. Compromise<br/>enables account takeover and PII disclosure. |
| JWT Session Tokens | Confidential | `RS256`-signed JWTs issued on login, stored in<br/>browser `localStorage`, and transmitted on<br/>every request via the `Authorization` header.<br/>The signing private key is hardcoded in<br/>`lib/insecurity.ts`, making every token<br/>forgeable by any party who reads the source. |
| Order and Delivery Records | Confidential | Customer order documents (order ID, items,<br/>total, delivery address) stored in the<br/>MarsDB `ordersCollection`. Linked to user<br/>addresses in the SQLite Addresses table.<br/>Disclosure reveals purchasing behavior and<br/>home addresses. |
| MarsDB Document Collections | Confidential | Embedded MarsDB (MongoDB-compatible)<br/>collections for product reviews and customer<br/>orders. Initialized in `data/mongodb.ts`; the<br/>reviews collection is vulnerable to NoSQL<br/>operator injection via \`$where` queries in<br/>`routes/chat.ts`. |
| Customer Feedback and Product Reviews | Internal | User-submitted feedback (comment, rating,<br/>contact) in the SQLite Feedbacks table and<br/>product reviews (message, author) in MarsDB.<br/>While often publicly displayed,<br/>unauthenticated write access and NoSQL<br/>injection risks make integrity a concern. |
| Challenge Definitions and Vulnerable Code<br/>Snippets | Internal | Training challenge metadata (names,<br/>descriptions, difficulty, flags), vulnerable<br/>code snippet files under<br/>data/static/codefixes/, and challenge-state<br/>records in the SQLite Challenges table.<br/>Exposure enables challenge bypass but is<br/>intentional by design. |

## 6. Security Architecture

This chapter is organized by security-control category. The architecture section avoids artificial control IDs and finding-ID columns in overview tables. Findings are listed only where the affected control is described.

_[§6](#6-security-architecture) schema v2 (13-section control-category layout). Cataloged controls: 21 total - 0 adequate, 6 partial, 6 weak, 5 unsafe, 4 missing. Linked threats: 50._

**How to read the verdicts.** Every control category (and every sub-control below it) carries exactly one status. The two red verdicts do **not** mean the same thing - this is the distinction that decides what you have to do about a finding:

| Status | Meaning | What it asks of you |
|----------|------------------------------------|------------------------|
| 🟢 Adequate | Control is present and sound | Nothing - keep it |
| 🟡 Partial | Present, but with meaningful gaps | Close the gap |
| 🟠 Weak | Present, but has exploitable gaps | Strengthen it |
| 🔴 Unsafe | **Present and relied upon, but defeated /<br/>trivially bypassable** | **Fix the existing control** |
| 🔴 Missing | **Control was never built** | **Add the control** |
| - | Not applicable to this codebase | - |

So "🔴 Unsafe" on a control category does *not* mean the control is absent - it means the control exists but does not hold (e.g. an `MD5` password hash, a raw-SQL query path, a hardcoded signing key). "🔴 Missing" is reserved for controls that were never built (e.g. no `Content-Security-Policy` header).

### 6.2 Identity and Authentication Controls

<a id="ctrl-identity-and-authentication-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) unconfirmed - 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`), 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Security-question password reset without secondary verification, 🔴 [F-023](#f-023) — Unauthenticated admin configuration exposure (`server.ts:606`), +1 · [tb-2](#tb-2) refuted - 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel, 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Unauthorized Information Broadcast (`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:29`) · [tb-3](#tb-3) refuted - 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass (`routes/login.ts:34`)


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-004](#w-004)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.2.1 Two-Factor Authentication](#621-two-factor-authentication)

**Implemented controls:** `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (hardcoded RSA private key), `lib/insecurity.ts:54` (`jwt.sign` with `RS256`), `lib/insecurity.ts:52` (`expressJwt` verification middleware); `lib/insecurity.ts:38` (`MD5` hash function applied to credentials), `models/user.ts:27` (password column storing `MD5` hashes); `server.ts:309-312` (unauthenticated upload/memory routes), `server.ts:406` (unauthenticated feedback POST), `server.ts:606-607` (unauthenticated admin info routes); `routes/2fa.ts` (TOTP setup and verification), `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts` (2FA event handling); `server.ts:204` (URL deduplication regex), `server.ts:411-413` (trim on registration fields).

**Assessment:** Authentication controls exist across the stack - `RS256` token signing, `MD5` credential storage, TOTP enrollment, and route middleware - but every critical-path control is defeated: the signing key is committed to source, the hash algorithm lacks salt and uses `MD5`, login and OAuth flows accept user-supplied email as a SQL string, and several administrative routes bypass the `expressJwt` middleware entirely. Each successful flow terminates in the server issuing a session token; the signing, validation, storage, and lifecycle of that token are described in [§6.3 Session and Token Controls](#63-session-and-token-controls).
