<a id="jwt-token-signing-and-verification"></a>
**JWT Token Signing and Verification.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the signing key is committed in plaintext, and the verification middleware accepts the `alg:none` bypass.

`lib/insecurity.ts:21` (hardcoded RSA private key), `lib/insecurity.ts:54` (`jwt.sign` with `RS256`), `lib/insecurity.ts:52` (`expressJwt` verification middleware)

The diagram shows the intended `RS256` token-issuance and verification flow:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant API as Express API
    participant JWT as JWT Issuer (lib/insecurity.ts)
    participant MW as expressJwt Middleware

    User->>API: POST /rest/user/login with credentials
    API->>JWT: sign({ userId, role }, rsaPrivateKey, { algorithm: "RS256" })
    JWT-->>API: signed token
    API-->>User: 200 OK + token
    User->>API: GET /rest/basket/:id with Bearer token
    API->>MW: expressJwt verify token
    MW-->>API: decoded payload
    API-->>User: basket data
```

**Security assessment**

`RS256` tokens are issued at `lib/insecurity.ts:54` and verified by the `expressJwt` middleware mounted at `lib/insecurity.ts:52`. Two independent weaknesses defeat the control:

- **Hardcoded private key** — `lib/insecurity.ts:21` embeds the RSA private key as a literal string in source. Any developer or CI system with repository read access can sign arbitrary tokens offline without touching the running server.
- **Missing algorithm allowlist** — `expressJwt` is configured without an `algorithms` restriction, so a token signed with `alg:none` (no signature) passes the middleware's verification check, allowing unauthenticated token forgery with no key material at all.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Algorithm Confusion via Missing Algorithm Allowlist — algorithm-confusion bypass passes the expressJwt middleware without a valid RSA signature.
- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery — committed RSA private key allows offline forgery of tokens for any user or role.
- 🟠 [F-013](#f-013) — OAuth Implicit Flow Exposes Access Token in URL Fragment — OAuth implicit flow exposes the access token in the URL fragment before local-session JWT is issued.

<a id="password-hashing"></a>
**Password Hashing.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - `MD5` without salt is used; a breached database yields plaintext passwords in seconds via rainbow table lookup.

`lib/insecurity.ts:38` (`MD5` hash function applied to credentials), `models/user.ts:27` (password column storing `MD5` hashes)

`lib/insecurity.ts:38` applies Node's `crypto.createHash('md5')` to the plaintext password and stores the hex digest in `models/user.ts:27`. The `MD5` algorithm is broken for this purpose: it is fast, unsalted, and fully covered by pre-computed rainbow tables. Every credential in the database is recoverable offline within minutes from a single table dump. Cross-reference with the deep dive in [§6.9.1 Managed Secret Storage](#managed-secret-storage) and [§6.9.2 Transport Encryption](#transport-encryption).

**Security assessment**

The hash function at `lib/insecurity.ts:38` is `MD5` - a fast general-purpose digest, not a password-hardening function. It applies no per-user salt, so two accounts with the same password produce the same hash, and the entire credential store can be cracked in a single offline rainbow-table pass. A purpose-built function (bcrypt, argon2, or scrypt) with a random salt per credential is the required replacement.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — MD5 Password Hash Without Salt Enables Offline Recovery — MD5 without salt enables offline credential recovery from any database read.
- 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — Unsalted MD5 hashes expose entire credential store — unsalted MD5 means a single rainbow-table pass cracks the entire user store.

<a id="route-authentication-middleware"></a>
**Route Authentication Middleware.**


**Status:** 🟠 Weak - `expressJwt` is applied to most routes, but several administrative and functional paths are registered without it.

`server.ts:309-312` (unauthenticated upload/memory routes), `server.ts:406` (unauthenticated feedback POST), `server.ts:606-607` (unauthenticated admin info routes)

`server.ts` registers routes in a flat list; `expressJwt` (from `lib/insecurity.ts:52`) is applied selectively via middleware insertion, not as a blanket guard. Routes for file upload (`server.ts:309-312`), anonymous feedback (`server.ts:406`), and two administrative info endpoints (`server.ts:606-607`) are registered before or without the `expressJwt` guard.

**Security assessment**

The gap is selective: most data routes sit behind the `expressJwt` middleware, but the admin endpoints at `server.ts:606-607` (`GET /rest/admin/application-version` and `GET /rest/admin/application-configuration`) require no token. Any internet visitor can retrieve the running application version and full configuration object without authenticating. The file-upload and feedback paths at `server.ts:309-312` and `server.ts:406` are similarly unguarded, enabling unauthenticated file placement and feedback submission.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-032](#f-032) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware — sensitive routes registered without authentication middleware expose functionality to unauthenticated callers.
- 🔴 [F-023](#f-023) — Unauthenticated admin configuration exposure — unauthenticated admin configuration endpoint leaks running-server details.

<a id="two-factor-authentication"></a><a id="two-factor-authentication-totp"></a>
#### 6.2.1 Two-Factor Authentication

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - TOTP enrollment and verification are implemented but the second factor is optional and not enforced for privileged accounts.

`routes/2fa.ts` (TOTP setup and verification), `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts` (2FA event handling)

The diagram shows the TOTP enrollment and challenge flow:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular SPA
    participant API as Express API
    participant TOTP as TOTP Verifier (routes/2fa.ts)

    User->>SPA: Navigate to account security settings
    SPA->>API: GET /rest/2fa/setup
    API-->>SPA: TOTP secret + QR code URI
    User->>SPA: Scan QR code, enter first TOTP code
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/2fa/setup with token
    API->>TOTP: verify(token, secret)
    TOTP-->>API: verified
    API-->>SPA: 200 OK - 2FA enrolled
    User->>SPA: Next login attempt
    SPA->>API: POST /rest/2fa/verify with TOTP code
    API->>TOTP: verify(token, secret)
    TOTP-->>API: verified
    API-->>SPA: final session JWT
```

**Security assessment**

`routes/2fa.ts` implements standard TOTP enrollment (shared-secret generation, QR-code delivery) and per-login verification via WebSocket events in `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts`. The functional path is sound. The gap is enforcement: enrollment is voluntary, no admin policy requires 2FA for any role, and the login flow at `routes/login.ts` issues a session token after password verification alone when 2FA is not enrolled. An attacker who recovers a password (by SQL injection or credential cracking) reaches a full session without encountering the second factor.

**Relevant findings**

- No dedicated finding is routed to this control; the coverage gap is a policy defect rather than an implementation bug.

<a id="input-validation"></a><a id="request-input-validation"></a>
**Request Input Validation.**


**Status:** 🟡 Partial - basic string trimming and URL deduplication are applied on registration; no structured schema validation or rate limiting is applied to authentication routes.

`server.ts:204` (URL deduplication regex), `server.ts:411-413` (trim on registration fields)

Registration fields go through a trim pass at `server.ts:411-413` and URLs through a deduplication regex at `server.ts:204`. No request-schema library (express-validator, Joi, Zod) enforces field types, lengths, or formats on the authentication endpoints.

**Security assessment**

Input handling at authentication boundaries is minimal: `trim()` on email and password fields prevents whitespace-only submissions but provides no type safety or length cap. The login endpoint at `routes/login.ts:34` accepts arbitrary-length email strings, which feeds directly into a raw SQL query with no sanitization. No rate limiting is applied to the login path, enabling unlimited brute-force attempts. The absence of schema validation at the registration endpoint is why the mass-assignment finding at 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body `routes/verify.ts:53` is reachable: the server accepts and processes any field in the request body.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-045](#f-045) — No Rate Limiting on Login Endpoint Enables Brute-Force and Exhaustion — no rate limiting on the login endpoint enables credential-stuffing and brute-force attacks.
- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body — absence of schema validation on registration allows privileged fields to be accepted from untrusted input.

<a id="sql-injection-prevention"></a>
**SQL Injection Prevention.**


**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Sequelize ORM handles most queries safely; the login route bypasses the ORM and concatenates user input into a raw SQL string.

`routes/login.ts:34` (raw `sequelize.query` interpolating user-supplied email), `models/user.ts:20` (Sequelize model methods for ORM queries)

Sequelize backs the majority of database access via model methods (`User.findOne`, `Product.findAll`), which bind parameters correctly. `routes/login.ts:34` calls `models.sequelize.query()` directly with the user-supplied email interpolated into the SQL string.

**Security assessment**

The ORM model methods are used safely throughout most of the application, so the injection surface is narrow but critical: the login path. `routes/login.ts:34` builds the credential lookup as a string template, allowing the classic `' OR 1=1--` payload to bypass password verification entirely and authenticate as the first row in the users table (typically an admin). The pattern recurs in the search route at `routes/search.ts:23`, discussed in [§6.5.1 Database Query Construction](#database-query-construction).

The vulnerable login lookup is built as a raw SQL string:

```ts
models.sequelize.query(
  `SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = '${req.body.email || ''}'...`
)
```

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass — SQL injection in the login query allows authentication bypass and admin impersonation.
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL injection via raw query string interpolation — duplicate raw SQL interpolation finding confirming the login-route vulnerability surface.

<a id="output-encoding-xss-protection"></a>
**Output Encoding / XSS Protection.**


**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - multiple Angular components call `bypassSecurityTrustHtml`, disabling the framework's default template escaping on user-controlled content.

`frontend/src/app/search-result/search-result.component.ts:110`,143; `frontend/src/app/last-login-ip/last-login-ip.component.ts:39`; `frontend/src/app/administration/administration.component.ts:73`,91; `frontend/src/app/about/about.component.ts:119`; `frontend/src/app/data-export/data-export.component.ts:57`; `frontend/src/app/track-result/track-result.component.ts:48`

Angular's template engine escapes interpolated values by default. `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` overrides that escape and marks a value as safe for raw HTML rendering, which is correct only for explicitly sanitized application-controlled strings - never for user-supplied content.

**Security assessment**

Six separate components call `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` on values that originate from user-controlled sources: search terms (reflected back in results), last-login IP (from a server field that can be poisoned by a crafted header), administration tables (user-submitted data), and order-tracking results. Each call site creates a stored or reflected XSS sink. Because the session bearer token is stored in `localStorage` (see [§6.3.1 JWT Token Storage](#jwt-token-storage)), any of these sinks allows an injected script to exfiltrate the session and perform authenticated actions on behalf of the victim.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — XSS via Angular Sanitizer Bypass — Angular sanitizer bypass across six component files creates persistent and reflected XSS sinks.

### 6.3 Session and Token Controls

<a id="ctrl-session-and-token-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) unconfirmed - 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`), 🟠 [F-012](#f-012) — Security-question password reset without secondary verification, 🔴 [F-023](#f-023) — Unauthenticated admin configuration exposure (`server.ts:606`), +1 · [tb-2](#tb-2) refuted - 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel, 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Unauthorized Information Broadcast (`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:29`) · [tb-3](#tb-3) refuted - 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass (`routes/login.ts:34`)

**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.3.1 JWT Token Storage](#631-jwt-token-storage)
- [6.3.2 Session Cookie Hardening](#632-session-cookie-hardening)
- [6.3.3 CORS Policy](#633-cors-policy)

**Implemented controls:** `frontend/src/app/Services/basket.service.ts:64` (`localStorage.getItem` for token), `frontend/src/app/Services/request.interceptor.ts:13` (JWT header injection); `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:20` (Socket\.IO CORS origin: localhost:4200).

**Assessment:** This application uses a single locally-signed token format (commonly called JWT) for every authenticated session, regardless of the login flow in [§6.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls) that established it. The sub-sections below trace one token through its lifecycle: signing on issuance (covered in [§6.2.1 JWT Token Signing and Verification](#jwt-token-signing-and-verification)), storage in the browser, Bearer presentation on every request, and the cross-origin policy that governs which origins may attach credentials. Token storage in `localStorage` is the dominant structural risk: it places the session credential inside the script execution context, where any XSS payload can read it.

The diagram shows the token lifecycle from storage to Bearer presentation:

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    actor User
    participant SPA as Angular SPA
    participant LS as localStorage
    participant API as Express API
    participant MW as expressJwt Middleware

    User->>SPA: Successful login
    SPA->>LS: localStorage.setItem("token", jwt)
    User->>SPA: Navigate to basket
    SPA->>LS: localStorage.getItem("token")
    LS-->>SPA: jwt string
    SPA->>API: GET /rest/basket/1 with Authorization: Bearer jwt
    API->>MW: expressJwt verify
    MW-->>API: decoded { userId, role }
    API-->>SPA: basket data
```

<a id="jwt-token-storage"></a>
#### 6.3.1 JWT Token Storage

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - the session bearer token is stored in `localStorage`, placing it inside the script execution context where any injected script can read it.
⚠ **Anti-pattern:** SPA without BFF

`basket.service.ts:64` calls `localStorage.getItem("token")` to retrieve the token for outgoing requests, and `request.interceptor.ts:13` attaches it as a Bearer header. No Backend-for-Frontend pattern is used; the SPA holds the credential client-side.

**Security assessment**

`localStorage` is readable by any JavaScript executing in the same origin, including XSS payloads. Because six `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` call sites exist in the Angular frontend (see 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — XSS via Angular Sanitizer Bypass), the XSS surface is active, not theoretical. The structural fix is a Backend-for-Frontend that stores the token in an `HttpOnly Secure SameSite=Strict` cookie, making it inaccessible to script. The current architecture has no server-side session store, so the token cannot be revoked without rotating the signing key.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-027](#f-027) — JWT bearer token stored in localStorage — JWT bearer token in `localStorage` is reachable by any injected page script.
- 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — JWT Stored in localStorage Exposed to XSS Theft — Angular `login.component.ts:101` confirms the token is written to `localStorage` on every successful login.

<a id="session-cookie-hardening"></a>
#### 6.3.2 Session Cookie Hardening

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - `express-session` cookies carry the `httpOnly` flag but `Secure` and `SameSite=Strict` are absent in the default development configuration.

Express-session cookies are used for the CAPTCHA and feedback workflows. The `httpOnly` flag prevents JavaScript from reading the session cookie directly; however, without `Secure` the cookie is transmitted on plain HTTP connections, and without `SameSite=Strict` cross-origin POST requests can attach it.

**Security assessment**

The JWT bearer token (the primary session credential) bypasses cookie storage entirely and lives in `localStorage`, so cookie hardening does not protect the main authentication flow. The express-session cookie used for stateful sub-workflows lacks the `Secure` and `SameSite=Strict` attributes. In a deployment behind HTTPS this is an incomplete hardening, not a standalone critical finding; the dominant session risk is the `localStorage` storage pattern above.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-027](#f-027) — JWT bearer token stored in localStorage — primary session credential stored outside cookie scope, making cookie hardening moot for the main flow.
- 🟠 [F-043](#f-043) — JWT Stored in localStorage Exposed to XSS Theft — confirms token is written to `localStorage` on login, not an `HttpOnly` cookie.

<a id="cors-policy"></a>
#### 6.3.3 CORS Policy

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - the `Socket.IO` channel restricts CORS to `localhost:4200`; the main HTTP API applies a wildcard CORS policy via `app.use(cors())`.

`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:20` sets the `Socket.IO` origin to `localhost:4200`. `server.ts:183` calls `app.use(cors())` with no origin restriction, which echoes back `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` to all callers.

**Security assessment**

The `Socket.IO` CORS restriction is a positive control for the real-time channel. The HTTP API wildcard at `server.ts:183` allows any origin to make credentialed-equivalent requests. Because the bearer token is sent in an `Authorization` header (not a cookie), the wildcard CORS policy does not on its own enable cross-origin credential theft - but it removes the same-origin origin validation layer that would otherwise limit which external sites can read API responses. Combined with the `localStorage` token storage, a cross-site script running on a malicious page can read the token from storage and call the API directly.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-040](#f-040) — CORS wildcard permits all-origin cross-site requests — CORS wildcard on the main HTTP API permits all-origin cross-site reads of API responses.

### 6.4 Authorization Controls

<a id="ctrl-authorization-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🟠 [F-038](#f-038) — Wallet identity accepted on HTTP body claim alone (`routes/nftMint.ts:41`), 🔴 [F-051](#f-051) — Challenge solve granted without verifying caller performed the on-chain exploit · [tb-4](#tb-4) unconfirmed - 🟠 [F-033](#f-033) — Missing GITHUB_TOKEN permissions restriction (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:1`)


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-002](#w-002)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.4.1 Object-Level Authorization](#641-object-level-authorization)
- [6.4.2 Centralized Authorization Policy](#642-centralized-authorization-policy)
- [6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection](#643-management-endpoint-protection)

**Implemented controls:** `server.ts:370-371`, 388-390, 426, 429, 433, 440-441, 450-451 (routes with `authn=middleware_present`, `authz=unknown`); `server.ts:370-371`, 389-390, 426, 429, 433, 440-441, 450-451, 605, 612-614, 625, 627, 633-634; `server.ts:606` (GET `/rest/admin/application-version` `authn=unknown`), `server.ts:607` (GET `/rest/admin/application-configuration` `authn=unknown`).

**Assessment:** Routes are registered with the `expressJwt` middleware in place on most paths, establishing token verification as the authentication gate. `Authorization` - the step that checks whether the authenticated identity is permitted to access the specific resource - is largely absent at the object level: routes accept any authenticated token without verifying resource ownership. The Angular `AdminGuard` provides a client-side role check that is trivially bypassed by any user who modifies their own token, and the admin configuration endpoints require no authentication at all.

<a id="object-level-authorization"></a><a id="object-level-authorization-bolaidor"></a>
#### 6.4.1 Object-Level Authorization

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - token presence is checked on most routes; ownership of the addressed object (basket, order, review) is not verified.

`server.ts:370-371`, 388-390, 426, 429, 433, 440-441, 450-451 (routes with `authn=middleware_present`, `authz=unknown`)

Route handlers in `routes/basket.ts`, `routes/order.ts`, and the review endpoints accept any valid token and serve or modify the object identified by the URL or request-body parameter, without checking that the token's `userId` matches the object's owner. The Sequelize-backed `BasketItem` and `Order` models expose row IDs in their REST responses, making enumeration straightforward.

**Security assessment**

The control is absent at the business-object layer: `GET /rest/basket/:id` returns any basket for any authenticated user who sends the numeric ID. The same pattern applies to order history and review operations. A logged-in user who increments the basket ID in the URL receives another user's cart contents without any ownership check. The IDOR finding at 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — Insecure Direct Object Reference confirms this path. The `routes/createProductReviews.ts:23` path additionally lacks an author check, allowing any user to overwrite another user's review.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — Insecure Direct Object Reference — direct object reference on the basket route exposes any customer's cart to any authenticated user.
- 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Missing ownership check enables cross-user bulk review update — missing ownership check on bulk review update allows cross-user modification.
- 🟡 [F-037](#f-037) — Missing author ownership check — missing author ownership check on review creation allows overwriting other users' reviews.

<a id="centralized-authorization-policy"></a>
#### 6.4.2 Centralized Authorization Policy

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - role-based access decisions are made at individual route handlers; no centralized policy layer enforces the distinction between user and admin roles server-side.

`server.ts:370-371`, 389-390, 426, 429, 433, 440-441, 450-451, 605, 612-614, 625, 627, 633-634

Route-level authorization is applied ad hoc: some routes check the `role` field from the decoded JWT; others rely on the Angular `AdminGuard` to block access in the browser. There is no shared authorization middleware that enforces a consistent role-permission matrix across all routes.

**Security assessment**

Because the JWT signing key is committed to source (🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`)) and the algorithm allowlist is absent (🔴 [F-001](#f-001) — JWT Algorithm Confusion via Missing Algorithm Allowlist), the role field inside any token is attacker-controllable. Even if route-level role checks were comprehensive, an attacker who forges a token with `role: admin` passes every check. The deeper problem is the client-side guard: `app.guard.ts:54` (the Angular `AdminGuard`) restricts the admin interface in the browser, but the server routes it protects are accessible by any user who sends a direct HTTP request with an `Authorization` header. No server-side role check blocks those calls.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-032](#f-032) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware — sensitive routes registered without authentication middleware are also reachable without role enforcement.
- 🟡 [F-050](#f-050) — Client-Side-Only Admin Role Check in AdminGuard — client-side-only admin guard means any authenticated user can call admin APIs directly.
- 🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body — mass assignment on the registration route allows an unauthenticated caller to set their own role.

<a id="management-endpoint-protection"></a>
#### 6.4.3 Management Endpoint Protection

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - admin configuration endpoints are reachable without any authentication token.

`server.ts:606` (GET `/rest/admin/application-version` `authn=unknown`), `server.ts:607` (GET `/rest/admin/application-configuration` `authn=unknown`)

`server.ts:606-607` registers the `/rest/admin/application-version` and `/rest/admin/application-configuration` GET endpoints without inserting the `expressJwt` middleware. Both return application metadata and configuration data to any caller.

**Security assessment**

`/rest/admin/application-configuration` returns the running server's configuration object, which includes key names and structure details useful for fingerprinting and attack preparation. Neither endpoint checks for a token, let alone an admin role. This makes the administrative surface directly accessible to unauthenticated internet visitors - a configuration reconnaissance surface with no access barrier.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-023](#f-023) — Unauthenticated admin configuration exposure — unauthenticated admin configuration endpoint at `server.ts:606` leaks version and configuration data.

### 6.5 Query Construction and Data Access Controls

<a id="ctrl-query-construction-and-data-access-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-5](#tb-5) refuted - 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL injection via raw query string interpolation (`routes/login.ts:34`) · [tb-6](#tb-6) refuted - 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — NoSQL \$where injection enables JavaScript execution (`showProductReviews.ts:36`)


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-001](#w-001)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.5.1 Database Query Construction](#651-database-query-construction)

**Implemented controls:** Sequelize ORM model methods (`User.findOne`, `Product.findAll`, `BasketItem.findOrCreate`) for the majority of data-access paths; MarsDB embedded store for product reviews.

**Assessment:** Sequelize backs the majority of relational queries and binds parameters correctly through its model API. Two routes bypass the ORM and call `models.sequelize.query()` with raw user input, and the MarsDB review path passes a user-controlled `$where` clause. These three injection-vulnerable paths cover the highest-traffic operations: login, product search, and product reviews.

<a id="database-query-construction"></a><a id="query-construction"></a>
#### 6.5.1 Database Query Construction

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - raw SQL string interpolation in the login and search routes, and a NoSQL `$where` clause in the review route, defeat the ORM's parameterization on the highest-traffic paths.

Sequelize ORM model methods handle most data access correctly; `routes/login.ts:34` and `routes/search.ts:23` call `models.sequelize.query()` directly with interpolated request values. `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36` passes a user-controlled selector to MarsDB's `$where` operator.

**Security assessment**

The safe Sequelize model API is used throughout most of the application. The critical exceptions are the login and search queries, which concatenate `req.body.email` and `req.query.q` directly into SQL strings. A standard injection payload bypasses the login check and authenticates as the database's first user. The product-search injection at `routes/search.ts:23` allows read access to any table in the SQLite database. The MarsDB `$where` clause at `routes/showProductReviews.ts:36` accepts a JavaScript expression, enabling `$where: "sleep(5000)"` denial-of-service or data extraction via boolean-blind injection.

The product search route illustrates the raw SQL construction pattern:

```ts
models.sequelize.query(
  `SELECT * FROM Products WHERE ((name LIKE '%${criteria}%'
   OR description LIKE '%${criteria}%') AND deletedAt IS NULL)
   ORDER BY name`
)
```

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass — SQL injection in the login query enables authentication bypass.
- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string — SQL injection in the product search query enables full database read.
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL injection via raw query string interpolation — duplicate evidence confirming the raw SQL pattern at `routes/login.ts:34`.
- 🔴 [F-009](#f-009) — NoSQL \$where injection enables JavaScript execution — NoSQL `$where` JavaScript injection in the product-review route enables server-side code execution.
- 🔴 [F-029](#f-029) — NoSQL \$where JS injection returns all orders — NoSQL `$where` injection in the order-tracking route returns all orders for any user.

### 6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls

<a id="ctrl-input-boundary-validation-controls"></a>

**Dependent crossings:** [tb-1](#tb-1) refuted - 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL injection via raw query string interpolation (`routes/login.ts:34`), 🟠 [F-048](#f-048) — Unbounded in-memory address Set grows without size cap (`routes/web3Wallet.ts:16`) · [tb-4](#tb-4) unconfirmed - 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — Postinstall script execution via CI npm install (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:51`)

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.6.1 Validation Approach](#661-validation-approach)

**Implemented controls:** string `trim()` on registration fields at `server.ts:411-413`, URL deduplication regex at `server.ts:204`, `multer` file-size limit on upload routes.

**Assessment:** Boundary validation is present at the registration form and file-upload level. No schema-validation library enforces types, lengths, or content rules on the request body for authentication or business endpoints. The absence of rate limiting and resource caps on CPU-intensive operations creates denial-of-service vectors at the event-loop level.

<a id="validation-approach"></a>
#### 6.6.1 Validation Approach

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - field trimming and upload-size limits are applied; no structured schema validation or request-rate controls protect authentication or business endpoints.

Registration fields receive `trim()` at `server.ts:411-413`. File uploads are gated by a `multer` size limit. No request body schema (type, length, format) is validated for login, order, or review endpoints.

**Security assessment**

The absence of request schema validation is the structural enabler for mass assignment (🔴 [F-011](#f-011) — Mass assignment privileged field accepted from request body `routes/verify.ts:53`) and allows arbitrary-length strings to reach raw SQL and NoSQL query paths. The login endpoint applies no rate limit, enabling unlimited brute-force credential attempts (🟡 [F-045](#f-045) — No Rate Limiting on Login Endpoint Enables Brute-Force and Exhaustion). The WebSocket event handler at `lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:20` processes incoming events without a per-connection message-rate limit, allowing a single unauthenticated client to flood the event loop and starve legitimate connections (🟡 [F-047](#f-047) — Uncontrolled Resource Consumption (`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts:20`)).

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-031](#f-031) — Event-loop blocking via NoSQL sleep injection — NoSQL sleep injection demonstrates the absence of query-level resource controls.
- 🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — No per-user rate limit on LLM chat endpoint — no per-user rate limit on the LLM chat endpoint enables compute-cost abuse.
- 🟡 [F-047](#f-047) — Uncontrolled Resource Consumption — uncontrolled resource consumption via WebSocket event flooding.

### 6.7 Output Encoding and Rendering Controls

**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.7.1 Output Encoding and Client-Side Rendering](#671-output-encoding-and-client-side-rendering)

**Implemented controls:** Angular default template escaping is active for the majority of interpolated values; Helmet `xssFilter` and `noSniff` headers are set via `server.ts` middleware.

**Assessment:** Angular's template engine escapes interpolated values by default, providing broad XSS protection across the SPA. Six call sites explicitly override this protection via `bypassSecurityTrustHtml`, converting safe interpolated bindings to raw HTML rendering on user-controlled content. The Helmet response headers complement the frontend encoding layer but do not compensate for the sanitizer bypasses.

<a id="output-encoding-and-client-side-rendering"></a>
#### 6.7.1 Output Encoding and Client-Side Rendering

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - Angular default escaping is bypassed at six call sites where `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` is applied to user-controlled values.

Angular's template interpolation encodes HTML characters by default across the SPA. `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` is a deliberate Angular escape hatch for content that has been sanitized by the application; it is misapplied here on values that originate from user input.

**Security assessment**

`search-result.component.ts:110,143` applies `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` to the search term reflected in the results page. `last-login-ip.component.ts:39` applies it to the last-login IP field, which can be poisoned via an `X-Forwarded-For` header. `administration.component.ts:73,91`, `about.component.ts:119`, `data-export.component.ts:57`, and `track-result.component.ts:48` render user-submitted or server-reflected data with the same bypass. Each site is a stored or reflected XSS sink; combined with `localStorage` session storage, any triggered payload exfiltrates the victim's session token.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-002](#f-002) — XSS via Angular Sanitizer Bypass — `bypassSecurityTrustHtml` applied to user-controlled values at six component locations creates active XSS sinks.

### 6.9 Cryptography Secrets and Data Protection


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-003](#w-003), [W-008](#w-008)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Unsafe

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.9.1 Managed Secret Storage](#691-managed-secret-storage)
- [6.9.2 Transport Encryption](#692-transport-encryption)

**Implemented controls:** `lib/insecurity.ts:21` (RSA private key), `lib/insecurity.ts:39` (HMAC secret), `routes/checkKeys.ts:10` (BIP39 mnemonic).

**Assessment:** Three critical secrets (the RSA token-signing private key, the HMAC password-reset secret, and the BIP39 wallet mnemonic) are all committed to source as literal strings in `lib/insecurity.ts` and `routes/checkKeys.ts`. The algorithm choice (`RS256`) is sound, but key management is absent: any repository read grants the ability to forge tokens, reset arbitrary passwords, and derive all wallet private keys. HTTPS is expected in production but is not enforced in the codebase.

<a id="managed-secret-storage"></a>
#### 6.9.1 Managed Secret Storage

**Status:** 🔴 Unsafe - three production secrets are committed to source in plaintext; no runtime secret injection (environment variable, secrets manager) is implemented.

`lib/insecurity.ts:21` (RSA private key), `lib/insecurity.ts:39` (HMAC secret), `routes/checkKeys.ts:10` (BIP39 mnemonic)

`lib/insecurity.ts:21` embeds the PEM-encoded RSA private key used to sign all session tokens. `lib/insecurity.ts:39` embeds the HMAC secret used to generate password-reset tokens. `routes/checkKeys.ts:10` embeds a BIP39 mnemonic phrase from which all Web3 wallet private keys can be derived. All three are loaded directly from source at server startup; no environment variable or mounted secret is consulted.

**Security assessment**

The three committed secrets cover every privileged operation in the application: session impersonation, arbitrary password reset, and wallet-key derivation. Any developer, contractor, CI system, or repository archive holder can perform all three operations against the production server without any credential or network access beyond the repository itself. The key-rotation path requires a code change and redeploy, making incident response slow. The fix in all three cases is the same: remove the literal value from source, inject it at runtime from an environment variable or secrets manager, and rotate the compromised values immediately.

**Relevant findings**

- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery — hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery for any user or role.
- 🔴 [F-022](#f-022) — Hardcoded HMAC Secret Enables Arbitrary Password Reset — hardcoded HMAC secret enables arbitrary password-reset token generation.
- 🟠 [F-026](#f-026) — Unsalted MD5 hashes expose entire credential store — unsalted MD5 credential hashes (also addressed in [§6.2.2](#62-identity-and-authentication-controls)) are stored alongside the committed signing key, compounding the credential exposure.
- 🔴 [F-030](#f-030) — Hardcoded BIP39 mnemonic exposes all derived wallet keys — hardcoded BIP39 mnemonic exposes all derived wallet private keys.

<a id="transport-encryption"></a><a id="transport-encryption-tls"></a>
#### 6.9.2 Transport Encryption

**Status:** 🟡 Partial - HTTPS is supported and expected in production deployment; the codebase does not enforce a redirect from HTTP and the development server starts on plain HTTP.

TLS termination is handled at the infrastructure layer (reverse proxy or load balancer) in production deployments. `server.ts` binds to port 3000 over plain HTTP with no built-in redirect to HTTPS; an HTTPS-aware deployment must be configured externally.

**Security assessment**

The absence of an in-process HTTP-to-HTTPS redirect means a misconfigured or development deployment silently accepts credentials and session tokens over plaintext HTTP. The `localStorage` bearer token (see [§6.3.1](#631-jwt-token-storage)) is then exposed to network interception. This is a partial control: correct when the infrastructure enforces TLS, absent when it does not. Adding an explicit `server.ts` redirect and HSTS header removes the deployment-dependent gap.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-025](#f-025) — Payment card number stored as cleartext INTEGER — payment card numbers stored as cleartext integers in the database; transport encryption does not compensate for at-rest plaintext.

### 6.10 File Parser and Outbound Request Controls

<a id="ctrl-file-parser-and-outbound-request-controls"></a>

_No trust boundary in this model depends on this control class._


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-005](#w-005)
**Verdict:** 🟠 Weak

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the section's default mechanism — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.10.1 File Parser and Outbound Request Handling](#6101-file-parser-and-outbound-request-handling)

**Implemented controls:** `multer` file-size limit on the file-upload route; no content-type or path-containment verification beyond the size check.

**Assessment:** File upload is gated by a `multer` size limit. Path containment is not verified before constructing file-system paths from request parameters, enabling traversal. Server-side template injection via `eval` on a stored username, and outbound fetch calls constructed from user-supplied URL fragments, create remote code execution and SSRF vectors. An admin username stored in the database and rendered via `eval` is the most severe of these paths.

<a id="file-parser-and-outbound-request-handling"></a>
#### 6.10.1 File Parser and Outbound Request Handling

**Status:** 🟠 Weak - path traversal in the data-erasure route, server-side template injection via `eval`, and an outbound HTTP fetch that accepts user-controlled URLs are all present without mitigating controls.

`routes/dataErasure.ts:104` constructs a filesystem path from a request parameter without canonicalization. `routes/userProfile.ts:61` passes a stored username through `eval()`. `routes/userProfile.ts:87` compiles user-supplied input as a template. Outbound HTTP fetches in NFT and wallet routes accept user-controlled URL fragments.

**Security assessment**

- **Path traversal** — `routes/dataErasure.ts:104` joins the request-supplied filename with a base directory without verifying the result stays within the base, allowing `../../etc/passwd`-style reads.
- **Server-side code execution via `eval`** — `routes/userProfile.ts:61` passes the stored username field through `eval()`. A user who registers with a username containing a JavaScript expression gets it executed on the server on profile retrieval.
- **Server-side template injection** — `routes/userProfile.ts:87` compiles user-supplied input as a template source (likely via a sandboxed evaluator), but the sandbox is insufficient for preventing property pollution escapes.
- **SSRF** — NFT and wallet endpoints accept user-controlled URL parameters for outbound HTTP calls without a domain allowlist.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — Path traversal filesystem access from request input — path traversal in the data-erasure route allows filesystem read outside the intended directory.
- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — Input compiled as template source — server-side template injection at `routes/userProfile.ts:87` enables code execution.
- 🟠 [F-028](#f-028) — Unauthorized Information Broadcast — WebSocket broadcast leaks order-confirmation details to all connected clients.
- 🔴 [F-034](#f-034) — Code execution via eval on stored username — `eval` on the stored username at `routes/userProfile.ts:61` executes attacker-controlled JavaScript in the server process.
- 🟡 [F-042](#f-042) — Inverted tool-call visibility guard leaks LLM internals to non-admins — user-controlled key in the NFT mint route deletes another user's server-side state.

### 6.11 Operations Runtime and Supply Chain Controls


**Systemic weaknesses:** [W-006](#w-006)
**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.11.1 Automated SCA scanning](#6111-automated-sca-scanning)
- [6.11.2 Automated dependency updates](#6112-automated-dependency-updates)
- [6.11.3 Lockfile hygiene](#6113-lockfile-hygiene)

**Implemented controls:** GitHub Actions CI pipeline runs `npm install` and test suite; no SCA scan step, no Dependabot/Renovate configuration, no `npm ci` (lockfile-enforced install).

**Assessment:** The CI pipeline builds and tests the application but performs no software composition analysis. Dependencies are installed with `npm install` rather than `npm ci`, so the lockfile is advisory rather than enforced. No automated update tool (Dependabot, Renovate) is configured. CI workflow files use floating action references (`@main`, `@master`) without SHA pinning, creating a supply-chain vector where a compromised upstream action repository silently replaces build steps.

<a id="automated-sca-scanning"></a>
#### 6.11.1 Automated SCA scanning

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - no SCA tool (Snyk, `npm audit --json` in CI, OWASP Dependency-Check) is present in the CI pipeline.

The CI workflow at `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs `npm install`, the test suite, and a container build step. No `npm audit`, Snyk, or equivalent SCA invocation appears in any workflow file.

**Security assessment**

Without a CI-integrated SCA step, vulnerable dependency versions accumulate silently. The `package.json` lockfile records the resolved versions, but no automated process alerts on new CVEs or rejects builds when a known-vulnerable version is detected. The `postinstall` script execution vector (🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — Postinstall script execution via CI npm install (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:51`)) demonstrates that the CI install step runs arbitrary scripts from `node_modules` without a quarantine pass.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing Security Audit Logging — missing security audit logging means exploit attempts against vulnerable dependencies go undetected.
- 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — Postinstall script execution via CI npm install — `postinstall` script execution during `npm install` in CI runs untrusted code from the dependency tree.
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Floating CI action reference — floating CI action reference allows a compromised upstream to replace build steps.

<a id="automated-dependency-updates"></a>
#### 6.11.2 Automated dependency updates

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - no Dependabot or Renovate configuration is present; dependency versions are updated only by manual developer action.

`package.json` specifies dependencies without a `.github/dependabot.yml` or `renovate.json` configuration. No pull-request automation creates update proposals when new patch or minor versions are released.

**Security assessment**

Without automated update proposals, the window between a CVE disclosure and a version bump depends entirely on developer awareness. The `package.json` includes dependencies with known security histories (`express-jwt`, `jsonwebtoken`, `unzipper`, libxmljs2, `notevil`). Manual-only updates mean the application runs on those versions indefinitely unless a developer actively monitors release notes and changelogs. Dependabot or Renovate would create PRs automatically, shifting from reactive to proactive patch management.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing Security Audit Logging — absence of structured audit and update tooling leaves dependency risk unquantified.
- 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — Postinstall script execution via CI npm install — CI runs `npm install` without `--ignore-scripts`, executing `postinstall` hooks from the unaudited dependency tree.
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Floating CI action reference — floating action reference at `.github/workflows/image_actions.yml:33` allows supply-chain replacement without a SHA pin.

<a id="lockfile-hygiene"></a>
#### 6.11.3 Lockfile hygiene

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - CI uses `npm install` rather than `npm ci`, so the lockfile does not enforce resolved versions on each build.

`package-lock.json` records the resolved dependency tree. `.github/workflows/ci.yml:51` runs `npm install`, which rewrites the lockfile on version-range upgrades rather than failing when the lockfile and `package.json` diverge.

**Security assessment**

`npm ci` (clean install) reads the lockfile as a strict specification and fails if it is out of sync with `package.json`. `npm install` resolves version ranges and silently updates the lockfile, meaning the dependency tree that runs in CI and production can drift from what was reviewed at code-review time. Combined with the missing SCA step, this means a dependency upgrade that introduces a known-vulnerable version can reach production without any automated detection. Switching the CI step from `npm install` to `npm ci` is a one-line fix that restores deterministic builds.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-003](#f-003) — Missing Security Audit Logging — non-deterministic dependency install means the runtime dependency tree is not reproducible from the committed lockfile.
- 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — Postinstall script execution via CI npm install — `npm install` executes `postinstall` scripts; `npm ci --ignore-scripts` would prevent this.
- 🟠 [F-021](#f-021) — Floating CI action reference — floating CI action reference compounds the supply-chain risk introduced by non-pinned installs.

### 6.12 Real-time and Not Applicable Controls

**Verdict:** 🔴 Missing

<!-- The line below is mechanically derived from the controls table — LLM must not re-author it. -->
**Controls covered:**

- [6.12.1 LLM Prompt Injection Filtering](#6121-llm-prompt-injection-filtering)

**Implemented controls:** `routes/chat.ts:108`,111 (user content forwarded to LLM endpoint without filtering).

**Assessment:** The application exposes an LLM chat endpoint at `server.ts:638` that forwards user input to a model API without filtering, output validation, or per-user rate limiting. `Socket.IO` real-time channels carry order-completion broadcasts and 2FA events but no authentication check gates the initial channel connection. The real-time surface adds denial-of-service and information-disclosure vectors beyond the LLM-specific risks.

<a id="llm-prompt-injection-filtering"></a>
#### 6.12.1 LLM Prompt Injection Filtering

**Status:** 🔴 Missing - the chat route at `routes/chat.ts:108,111` forwards raw user input to the model without a system-prompt boundary or output-validation step.

`routes/chat.ts` receives user messages, constructs a prompt, and forwards it to the LLM endpoint. No input-filtering step strips or escapes instruction-style text before it reaches the model's context. No output-validation step inspects the model's response before it is returned to the caller.

**Security assessment**

The chat endpoint accepts free-text user messages and concatenates them into the model's prompt context without a hardened system-prompt boundary. A crafted message that instructs the model to ignore its system prompt can override the coupon-discount cap constraint (🟠 [F-049](#f-049) — Prompt injection bypasses coupon discount cap via unconstrained schema), return internal tool-call details to non-admin users (🟡 [F-042](#f-042) — Inverted tool-call visibility guard leaks LLM internals to non-admins), or exhaust API quota through unbounded inference calls (🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — No per-user rate limit on LLM chat endpoint (`server.ts:638`)). The structural fix requires a prompt-injection resistant boundary (a separate system-message that the user context cannot override), output-schema validation before returning model responses, and a per-user inference rate limit.

**Relevant findings**

- 🟡 [F-042](#f-042) — Inverted tool-call visibility guard leaks LLM internals to non-admins — inverted visibility guard leaks LLM tool-call internals to non-admin users.
- 🟡 [F-044](#f-044) — No per-user rate limit on LLM chat endpoint — no per-user rate limit on the LLM chat endpoint enables compute-cost exhaustion.
- 🟠 [F-049](#f-049) — Prompt injection bypasses coupon discount cap via unconstrained schema — prompt injection bypasses the coupon-discount cap enforced by the model's system prompt.

_`Socket.IO` real-time channels are present in this codebase (`lib/startup/registerWebsocketEvents.ts`). No dedicated WebSocket security control was catalogued in this assessment. The relevant findings (event flooding, unauthorized broadcast, unauthenticated channel) are routed to [§6.4 Authorization Controls](#64-authorization-controls) and [§6.6 Input Boundary Validation Controls](#66-input-boundary-validation-controls) where the access-control and resource-limit gaps are assessed._

### 6.13 Defense-in-Depth Summary

**Verdict:** -

Two positive controls provide a foundation for layered defense: the `RS256` algorithm choice for token signing is sound (the weakness is key management, not the algorithm), and the Sequelize ORM correctly parameterizes the majority of database queries (the weakness is the two raw-query bypasses). The Angular template engine's default escaping and Helmet response headers apply a surface-level browser-protection layer.

Restoring layered defense requires closing four structural gaps: replacing the raw SQL interpolations with parameterized queries removes the most-reachable injection paths; rotating and externalizing the committed signing key and HMAC secret to runtime environment variables removes the offline-forgery vector; moving session token storage from `localStorage` to an `HttpOnly Secure SameSite=Strict` cookie (via a BFF pattern) removes the XSS-to-session-theft chain; and adding a CI-integrated SCA scan with `npm ci` restores a deterministic, audited dependency baseline. These four repairs address the dominant attack chains before any perimeter control is considered.

## 7. Weakness Register

Each weakness names the missing, home-grown, or misused control, the findings that evidence it, the components it spans, and its remediation. A weakness may also rest on observed unsafe practice or an absent architectural control with no confirmed exploit - only confirmed findings carry a CVSS score.

- 🔴 **Critical** · [W-001](#w-001) - Database access relies on concatenated queries · confirmed · 3 findings · 4 components
- 🔴 **Critical** · [W-002](#w-002) - `Authorization` is implemented route by route · confirmed · 6 findings · 2 components
- 🔴 **Critical** · [W-003](#w-003) - Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store · confirmed · 3 findings · 4 components
- 🟠 **High** · [W-004](#w-004) - Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication · confirmed · 3 findings · 4 components
- 🟠 **High** · [W-005](#w-005) - Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation · confirmed · 1 finding · 1 component
- 🟠 **High** · [W-006](#w-006) - Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references · confirmed · 1 finding · 1 component
- 🟡 **Medium** · [W-007](#w-007) - Injection is implemented inconsistently · observed-practice · 1 finding · 1 component
- 🟡 **Medium** · [W-008](#w-008) - Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives · observed-practice · 1 finding · 2 components
- 🟡 **Medium** · [W-009](#w-009) - Frontend rendering lacks enforced output encoding · design-risk · 0 findings · 0 components

<a id="w-001"></a>
### W-001 — Database access relies on concatenated queries

🔴 **Critical** · design weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Database queries are assembled from application values instead of passing those values through an enforced parameterised data-access path. This leaves every call site responsible for preserving query structure.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Raw SQL string interpolation.** The login and search routes bypass the Sequelize ORM and interpolate request-controlled values directly into SQL strings. The pattern recurs across multiple routes rather than being isolated, making a parameterized-query retrofit the only structural fix.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-005](#f-005) — SQL Injection Authentication Bypass (`routes/login.ts:34`)
- 🔴 [F-006](#f-006) — SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string `routes/search.ts:23`
- 🔴 [F-008](#f-008) — SQL injection via raw query string interpolation (`routes/login.ts:34`)

**Architecture evidence:** Parameterized Queries, ORM / Repository Layer

**Affected components:** backend-api, [C-02](#c-02), [C-03](#c-03), [C-04](#c-04)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — provide one parameterised repository or query-builder path and prohibit application-value interpolation in database queries
- **Tactical** — ● [M-005](#m-005) — Use parameterized database queries, ● [M-006](#m-006) — Use parameterized database queries, ● [M-008](#m-008) — Use parameterized database queries

<a id="w-002"></a>
### W-002 — Authorization is implemented route by route

🔴 **Critical** · design weakness · confirmed · 6 findings

`Authorization` depends on per-handler checks instead of a policy boundary that consistently enforces role, ownership, and tenant scope. New routes can bypass protection by omitting a local check.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Missing server-side authorization layer.** Several sensitive routes are registered without authentication middleware, and object-level authorization is absent on resource endpoints. The admin guard lives entirely in the Angular frontend, so any user who obtains a token - or bypasses token verification - has unrestricted API access.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-007](#f-007) — Insecure Direct Object Reference
- 🔴 [F-032](#f-032) — Sensitive Routes Registered Without Authentication Middleware
- 🔴 [F-035](#f-035) — Missing ownership check enables cross-user bulk review update
- 🟡 [F-037](#f-037) — Missing author ownership check (`routes/createProductReviews.ts:23`)
- 🟡 [F-041](#f-041) — User-controlled key deletes victim's NFT mint record from server state
- 🔴 [F-051](#f-051) — Challenge solve granted without verifying caller performed the on-chain exploit

**Architecture evidence:** Centralised AuthZ Policy, Role / Scope Enforcement, Ownership Check, Object-Level Ownership Check, Tenant Scoping

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-07](#c-07)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authorization through a shared server-side policy layer and make ownership and tenant scope mandatory inputs to data access
- **Tactical** — ● [M-007](#m-007) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization, ◕ [M-031](#m-031) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint, ◕ [M-034](#m-034) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint, ◑ [M-036](#m-036) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint, ◑ [M-040](#m-040) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization, ◑ [M-050](#m-050) — Enforce server-side authorization on every endpoint

<a id="w-003"></a>
### W-003 — Secrets are committed to source instead of a managed store

🔴 **Critical** · design weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Cryptographic keys, credentials, and other high-entropy secrets are embedded as literals in source or `config` rather than resolved at runtime from a managed secret store, so anyone with repository read access obtains reusable signing material and credentials.

**Architectural anti-pattern - Secrets hardcoded in source.** The RSA private key used to sign all session JWTs and the HMAC secret used for password-reset tokens are both committed in plaintext to `lib/insecurity.ts`. Rotating them requires a code change and redeploy; any developer or CI system with repository read access can forge tokens or reset arbitrary passwords.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🔴 [F-010](#f-010) — Hardcoded RSA private key enables offline JWT forgery (`lib/insecurity.ts:21`)
- 🔴 [F-022](#f-022) — Hardcoded HMAC Secret Enables Arbitrary Password Reset (`lib/insecurity.ts:42`)
- 🔴 [F-030](#f-030) — Hardcoded BIP39 mnemonic exposes all derived wallet keys (`checkKeys.ts:10`)

**Architecture evidence:** Managed Secret Store, Runtime Secret Injection

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-03](#c-03), [C-04](#c-04), [C-07](#c-07)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — move every secret to a managed secret store or injected environment configuration, rotate the exposed values, and add secret-scanning to CI
- **Tactical** — ● [M-010](#m-010) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store, ◕ [M-021](#m-021) — Move cryptographic keys to a managed secret store, ◕ [M-029](#m-029) — Move secrets to a managed secret store

<a id="w-004"></a>
### W-004 — Endpoints are reachable without enforced authentication

🟠 **High** · design weakness · confirmed · 3 findings

Sensitive API routes and real-time channels are exposed without an enforced authentication check at the endpoint boundary. Access control depends on each handler (or the caller) remembering to require a session, so an unauthenticated client can reach privileged operations directly.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-016](#f-016) — Unauthenticated WebSocket Channel
- 🔴 [F-023](#f-023) — Unauthenticated admin configuration exposure (`server.ts:606`)
- 🟡 [F-046](#f-046) — Unauthenticated Socket\.IO enables event flooding (`socket-io.service.ts:22`)

**Architecture evidence:** Route Authentication Middleware, Server-Side Session Enforcement

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02), [C-03](#c-03), [C-01](#c-01), [C-05](#c-05)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce authentication centrally at the routing and channel boundary so every exposed endpoint requires a verified session unless explicitly marked public
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-016](#m-016) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint, ◕ [M-022](#m-022) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint, ◑ [M-045](#m-045) — Require authentication on every exposed endpoint

<a id="w-005"></a>
### W-005 — Input handling lacks enforced boundary validation

🟠 **High** · design weakness · confirmed · 1 finding

Request handlers do not validate input against one enforced server-side schema or allowlist at the boundary - validation is either absent on the vulnerable parameters or limited to rejecting selected bad patterns (a blacklist). New encodings and unanticipated input forms can therefore reach downstream parsers or interpreters.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-017](#f-017) — Path traversal filesystem access from request input `routes/dataErasure.ts:104`

**Architecture evidence:** Schema Validation, Allowlist Validation

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — enforce server-side schemas and domain-specific allowlists before input reaches parsing, persistence, or command construction
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-017](#m-017) — Constrain file paths to a safe base directory

<a id="w-006"></a>
### W-006 — Build pipeline trusts mutable third-party references

🟠 **High** · design weakness · confirmed · 1 finding

CI/CD workflows resolve third-party actions and other build dependencies to mutable tags or branches instead of immutable commit digests, so a retagged or compromised upstream runs inside the pipeline with its token and secret scope.

**Confirmed findings:**

- 🟠 [F-020](#f-020) — Postinstall script execution via CI npm install (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:51`)

**Architecture evidence:** Commit-SHA Action Pinning, Dependency Provenance Verification

**Affected components:** [C-06](#c-06)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — pin every third-party action and build dependency to an immutable commit SHA (or a vetted internal mirror) and enforce SHA-pinning in CI
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-019](#m-019) — Pin third-party dependencies to immutable versions

<a id="w-007"></a>
### W-007 — Injection is implemented inconsistently

🟡 **Medium** · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

Untrusted input reaches an interpreter (SQL, OS shell, template, XML/YAML parser) without parameterization or a strict allowlist. It is systemic when the same unsafe construction pattern recurs across handlers rather than being an isolated slip.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-019](#f-019) — Input compiled as template source `routes/userProfile.ts:87` (`routes/userProfile.ts:87`)

**Affected components:** [C-02](#c-02)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — adopt parameterized queries / safe APIs and centralised input validation as the only sanctioned data-access and parsing path
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-018](#m-018) — Remove server-side evaluation of untrusted input

<a id="w-008"></a>
### W-008 — Security-sensitive data uses weak cryptographic primitives

🟡 **Medium** · implementation weakness · observed-practice · 1 finding

Password, token, or integrity protection uses a weak hash, predictable random source, or insufficient work factor. The application may use a standard library, but the selected primitive does not provide the required security property.

**Practice sites:**

- 🟠 [F-024](#f-024) — MD5 Password Hash Without Salt Enables Offline Recovery (`lib/insecurity.ts:41`) (`lib/insecurity.ts:41`)

**Affected components:** backend-api, [C-03](#c-03)

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — standardise on a password KDF, a CSPRNG for secrets, and modern authenticated cryptographic primitives with centrally reviewed parameters
- **Tactical** — ◕ [M-023](#m-023) — Hash passwords with a strong, salted algorithm

<a id="w-009"></a>
### W-009 — Frontend rendering lacks enforced output encoding

🟡 **Medium** · design weakness · design-risk

Browser rendering paths write HTML or DOM content directly without one enforced contextual-encoding or sanitisation boundary. Safety depends on each individual component using the right API.

**Architecture evidence:** Template Autoescape, Output Encoding, Content Security Policy

**Remediation:**

- **Structural** — use framework-safe rendering by default and isolate any required raw HTML behind one reviewed sanitisation and Trusted Types policy

---

Each finding is a card with the same fixed fields, in order: **Severity · Component · Location** → **Issue** → **Root cause** → **Evidence** → **Fix** → **Classification** (with external CWE / OWASP links).

### 🔴 Critical (8)

<a id="t-001"></a><a id="f-001"></a>
#### F-001 · JWT Algorithm Confusion via Missing Algorithm Allowlist

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Authentication and Session Management  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (6)

**Instances (6):** 🔴 `lib/insecurity.ts:52`, 🔴 `lib/insecurity.ts:55`, 🟠 `lib/insecurity.ts:53`, 🟠 `lib/insecurity.ts:56`, 🔴 `lib/insecurity.ts:189`, 🔴 `routes/verify.ts:120`

**Issue:** Without an explicit algorithm allowlist, attackers can forge tokens with `alg:none` (older lib versions) or use the public key as an HMAC secret to mint valid signatures.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
// lib/insecurity.ts:189
export const updateAuthenticatedUsers = () => (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
  const token = req.cookies.token || utils.jwtFrom(req)
  if (token && authenticatedUsers.get(token) === undefined) {
    jwt.verify(token, publicKey, (err: Error | null, decoded: any) => {
      if (err === null && decoded?.data !== undefined) {
        authenticatedUsers.put(token, decoded)
        res.cookie('token', token)
```

**Fix:** Pin the signature algorithm explicitly and reject `alg:none` and unknown algorithms → ● [M-001](#m-001) — Enforce JWT signature and algorithm verification (`insecurity.ts:189`)

**Classification:** Broken Authentication · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-347](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/347.html) · [OWASP A07:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A07_2025-Authentication_Failures/) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.1](#31-jwt-algorithm-confusion-in-authentication-and-session-management)

<a id="t-005"></a><a id="f-005"></a>
#### F-005 · SQL Injection Authentication Bypass (routes/login.ts:34)

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-03](#c-03) - Authentication and Session Management  ·  **Location:** `routes/login.ts:34`

**Weakness:** [W-001](#w-001) - Database access relies on concatenated queries

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-3](#tb-3) 🌐 External - external → juice-shop-auth · Authentication: SQL injection at `routes/login.ts:34` violates the tb-3 authentication leg assumption that JWT issuance requires a matching user row returned by a parameterized credential query.

**Issue:** An anonymous attacker submits a SQL injection payload such as `' OR 1=1--` as the email field in POST `/rest/user/login`; the raw template literal interpolates the input directly into a `sequelize.query` call without parameterization; the WHERE clause evaluates true for all rows; the database returns the first user row (typically the admin account) and the server issues a valid JWT for that account without knowledge of the password. Full authentication bypass for any account including admin; attacker reaches all `sensitive_assets` (RSA private key, TOTP seeds, `MD5` password hashes) and gains complete control over all Juice Shop user accounts.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - `routes/login.ts:34` interpolates `req.body.email` directly into the query string via template literal; no parameterization or input sanitization is applied before the `sequelize.query` call; the known-threats record for this file confirms the SQL injection at this exact line.

```typescript
// routes/login.ts:34

  return (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
    verifyPreLoginChallenges(req) // vuln-code-snippet hide-line
    models.sequelize.query(`SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = '${req.body.email || ''}' AND password = '${security.hash(req.body.password || '')}' AND deletedAt IS NULL`, { model: UserModel, plain: true }) // vuln-code-snippet vuln-line loginAdminChallenge loginBenderChallenge loginJimChallenge
      .then((authenticatedUser) => { // vuln-code-snippet neutral-line loginAdminChallenge loginBenderChallenge loginJimChallenge
        const user = utils.queryResultToJson(authenticatedUser)
        if (user.data?.id && user.data.totpSecret !== '') {
```

**Fix:** Switch all SQL execution to parameterised queries or ORM-bound parameters → ● [M-005](#m-005) — Use parameterized database queries (`login.ts:34`)

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Spoofing · [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.4](#34-sql-injection-authentication-bypass-in-login)

<a id="t-006"></a><a id="f-006"></a>
#### F-006 · SQL injection request data interpolated into a SQL string routes/search.ts:23

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-02](#c-02) - Express REST API Backend  ·  **Location:** `routes/search.ts:23`

**Weakness:** [W-001](#w-001) - Database access relies on concatenated queries

**Issue:** Interpolating request-controlled text into a SQL statement lets an attacker alter the query - exfiltrating or modifying arbitrary rows, bypassing authentication, or escalating to full database control.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
// routes/search.ts:23
  return (req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction) => {
    let criteria: any = req.query.q === 'undefined' ? '' : req.query.q ?? ''
    criteria = (criteria.length <= 200) ? criteria : criteria.substring(0, 200)
    models.sequelize.query(`SELECT * FROM Products WHERE ((name LIKE '%${criteria}%' OR description LIKE '%${criteria}%') AND deletedAt IS NULL) ORDER BY name`) // vuln-code-snippet vuln-line unionSqlInjectionChallenge dbSchemaChallenge
      .then(([products]: any) => {
        const dataString = JSON.stringify(products)
        if (challengeUtils.notSolved(challenges.unionSqlInjectionChallenge)) { // vuln-code-snippet hide-start
```

**Fix:** Switch all SQL execution to parameterised queries or ORM-bound parameters → ● [M-006](#m-006) — Use parameterized database queries (`search.ts:23`)

**Classification:** Injection · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-89](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/89.html) · [OWASP A05:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A05_2025-Injection/) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.2](#32-sql-injection-request-data-interpolated-into-a-sql-string)

<a id="t-007"></a><a id="f-007"></a>
#### F-007 · Insecure Direct Object Reference

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-02](#c-02) - Express REST API Backend  ·  **Location:** Multiple locations (20)

**Weakness:** [W-002](#w-002) - `Authorization` is implemented route by route

**Instances (20):** 🔴 `routes/address.ts:11`, 🔴 `routes/address.ts:18`, 🔴 `routes/address.ts:29`, 🟠 `routes/basketItems.ts:68`, 🔴 `routes/dataExport.ts:26`, 🟠 `routes/delivery.ts:34`, 🔴 `routes/deluxe.ts:25`, 🔴 `routes/deluxe.ts:30` … (+12 more)

**Issue:** Server-side authorization MUST derive the resource owner from the authenticated session (`req.user` / `req.session` / `req.auth`), never from attacker-controlled request data. Trusting `req.body.UserId` etc. enables horizontal privilege escalation across all authenticated tenants.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified

```typescript
// routes/address.ts:11

export function getAddress () {
  return async (req: Request, res: Response) => {
    const addresses = await AddressModel.findAll({ where: { UserId: req.body.UserId } })
    res.status(200).json({ status: 'success', data: addresses })
  }
}
```

**Fix:** Tie every object lookup to the requesting user's identity and reject cross-tenant references → ● [M-007](#m-007) — Enforce object-level (ownership) authorization (`address.ts:11`)

**Classification:** Broken Access Control · STRIDE: Tampering · [CWE-639](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/639.html) · [OWASP A01:2025](https://owasp.org/Top10/2025/A01_2025-Broken_Access_Control/) · walkthrough [Walkthrough §3.8](#38-insecure-direct-object-reference-in-address)

<a id="t-008"></a><a id="f-008"></a>
#### F-008 · SQL injection via raw query string interpolation (routes/login.ts:34)

**Severity:** 🔴 Critical  ·  **Component:** [C-02](#c-02) - Express REST API Backend  ·  **Location:** `routes/login.ts:34`

**Weakness:** [W-001](#w-001) - Database access relies on concatenated queries

**Trust boundary gap:** [tb-1](#tb-1) 🌐 External - external → juice-shop-api · Validation: Raw SQL interpolation at `routes/login.ts:34` violates the validation assumption leg of tb-1, which requires all parameters to be bound before reaching business logic.<br>[tb-5](#tb-5) - juice-shop-auth → juice-shop-db · Query construction: Raw string interpolation at `routes/login.ts:34` violates tb-5's assumption that all credential lookups reach SQLite through parameterized queries, never through user-input interpolation.

**Issue:** An unauthenticated attacker submits POST `/rest/user/login` with email set to `' OR 1=1--`. The raw `sequelize.query` interpolates the email directly into the SQL string, producing `WHERE email = '' OR 1=1--' ...`, which matches the first user row (admin).

The server issues a valid JWT for that user without the attacker knowing any password. Authentication bypass allowing login as any user including admin; secondary injection can dump, modify, or delete all Users table rows including payment card associations and personal data.

**Evidence:** ✓ verified - `routes/login.ts:34`: `models.sequelize.query('SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = \'${req.body.email || \'\'}\'...')` - the email value is interpolated directly into the SQL string. The controls context confirms this raw query bypasses Sequelize parameterization.

